## Amendment record

Superseded wording is kept visible with its reason, so the decision trail survives. Same convention as `IntelliBooks-System-Specification.md`.

**When you write an amendment, go and strike the sentences it contradicts.** Added 2026-08-21. The amendment record grows and the body drifts, because a decision gets recorded here and the sentence it contradicts is left standing elsewhere in this document. 18.2b said the opposite of amendment 106 for three days, and 18.5b said a question was unanswered two hours after amendment 127 answered it.

### v1.4, 2026-07-28

Rows 40 to 43 above are also 28 July work, recorded under the v1.3 table before this section existed. Left where they are rather than moved, so no row number changes meaning.

| # | Section | Change | Why |
|---|---|---|---|
| 44 | 12.4 detail 3 | **The stated mechanism is corrected.** Superseded wording: "Desktop wrote the code into the name". It does not. Desktop writes `client:{code:c.code,name:c.name}`, the real name. The two registries hold **different names for the same client**: `IntelliBooks-Practice.json` has `{"name":"TEST","code":"TEST"}`, `clients.csv` has `Test`. Adds the consequence for the cloud version. | I inferred the mechanism from a filed sidecar reading `{"code":"TEST","name":"TEST"}` and was wrong about the cause. Flagged by the IntelliBooks session, confirmed against both registry files. The conclusion survives and gets stronger: never resolve a path from the note's `client.name`, because the two tools disagree on it. And a new consequence, which is why this is more than a footnote: Desktop writes `filed_path` as `Clients\TEST\...` from `safeName(c.name)` while the pipeline files the same client to `Clients\Test\`. `resolve_practice_path()` then calls `target.exists()`, which passes on Windows because NTFS is case-insensitive. **Test 41 therefore passes for the wrong reason**, and on S3 or Linux those are two folders. |
| 45 | 12.2 | Filename convention amended to what Desktop writes. Superseded wording: "Filename `{receipt_id}_{unix_ms}.json`". | Desktop writes the receipt id when the sidecar carried one, and otherwise the review image name with its extension stripped and any character outside `[A-Za-z0-9._-]` replaced by `-`. That is a fair reading of a section that permits a null `receipt_id`, and the pipeline only sorts on the name, so nothing breaks. Recorded because spec and shipped code disagreed, which is the drift this document exists to catch. |
| 46 | 12.2 | Records that `Resolutions\` sits inside OneDrive, and that this is a same-machine assumption rather than a property of the design. | Raised by the IntelliBooks session and worth keeping. Fine while Desktop and the pipeline run on one machine. On two, sync latency and conflict copies (`file-DESKTOP-ABC.json`) become the pipeline's problem, and `glob("*.json")` would pick them up. |
| 47 | 12.4, 17.4 | **Decided.** Leave both sidecar shapes on disk and make every reader tolerant, keyed on the discriminator: `corrected_by` means Desktop wrote it, `capture_date` means the pipeline did. | Paul's decision, 2026-07-28. Closes the question row 20 opened and 12.4 parked at step 10. Rewriting would be a write into a folder Desktop has just written to, with a new failure mode, for the benefit of no reader that exists yet. The discriminator makes tolerance a small testable rule rather than an open-ended one. |
| 48 | 12.3 step 2, 17.4 | The null-id filename fallback can miss. Flagged, not fixed. | Desktop sends the on-disk Review image name in `original_review_files`, and `file_review()` names that image through `_unique_path()`, which may have appended `-2`. `receipts.filename` holds the unsuffixed original, so the match fails and the note lands in `failed\`. Only bites when the id is absent **and** there was a name collision, and failing audibly into `failed\` is the designed behaviour for an unmatchable note. |
| 49 | 17.4 | Two flags from verifying the Desktop half, and one fix. | `write_review_file()` has no caller in `app.py` or `worker/`, and the `{stem}.review.json` name it writes is invisible to Desktop's `scanReview()`, which derives the image name by stripping `.review.json` and skips the item when no such file exists. Separately, `clients.csv` gave `Client_004` to both `Test` and `She Run's It! Ldn Ltd`, so anything keyed on `client_id` conflated two clients. **Fixed 2026-07-28** ahead of test 41 on Paul's instruction: `SHERUNSIT` becomes `Client_005`, which touched no existing row because all `Client_004` receipts belong to `TEST`. |
| 50 | 15 test 41, 16 | Test 41 covers **both** note actions, filed and discarded, and the `clients.csv` fix comes before it. | Paul's decisions, 2026-07-28. The discard branch has never run at all, and it is the cheaper half to get wrong. Two Review items means two OpenAI calls, accepted deliberately. Fixing the registry before generating new rows against it rather than after. |

| 51 | 12.2, 15 test 41 | **Terminology.** The note's `action` value is `discarded`, but **nothing in IntelliBooks Desktop says "discard".** The button is red and says **Delete**, on the Review row and in the Edit window, and it raises a browser confirm reading "Delete this receipt permanently from `Clients\{name}\Review\`? This cannot be undone." | Written after test-41 instructions phrased in the code's vocabulary confused Paul, which was my error and the exact failure the terminology rule in the handover exists to prevent. Anyone writing instructions for an operator names the button on screen; `discarded` is the value in the note and belongs in code and in this document, not in a walkthrough. Same class of problem as "the app": one thing, two names, and only one of them is visible to the person doing the work. |

| 52 | 17.4, and a change for the IntelliBooks session | **SUPERSEDED 2026-07-30 by amendment 68. Change D is cancelled and this row is history. The one fact in it that survives: a transaction has `amount` and `vat` and no net field, so net is always derived and a receipt's stated net is never carried across.** Original wording follows. **Decided.** A receipt whose own net, VAT and gross do not reconcile must produce a **warning at the point the transaction is posted**, in Desktop. Not a block, and nothing is carried into the books entry. | Paul's decision, 2026-07-29, taken after reviewing the branch test 41 did not exercise. What prompted it: a transaction has `amount` and `vat` and **no net field at all**, so net is always derived as the difference. Post a receipt reading net 21.50, VAT 4.30, gross 27.00 and the transaction is 27.00 with VAT 4.30, implied net 22.70. The receipt's stated net is dropped and the discrepancy resolves itself silently in favour of gross and VAT. That treatment is defensible, since gross is what left the bank and net is derived, but it was reached by omission rather than by decision. And the pipeline's own record of the disagreement, the `filed by decision in Desktop despite` line, lives in `extractions.validation_notes`, which Desktop never reads, so the caveat never reaches the person posting. The warning must use the pipeline's test exactly, `abs(round(net + vat, 2) - round(gross, 2)) > 0.02` and **only when all three are present**, so the two tools cannot disagree about the same receipt. `parseSidecar()` already carries `net` and `vat` through, so this works for auto-loaded receipts too. **Mechanism, decided the same day:** a confirm box on the single-receipt path, which the operator must acknowledge and may proceed through, and a **count** on the bulk path, because `bulkCashbook()` cannot ask per receipt and a toast that vanishes in 4.5 seconds is too weak for a figure that will not be questioned again. |
| 53 | 17.4, 13 | The blank-category-at-posting question was put alongside amendment 52 and **deliberately left open.** | Paul's decision, 2026-07-29. Recorded so nobody later reads it as an oversight, and so nobody bundles it into the VAT change on the assumption it was forgotten. The two questions sit at the same two functions, `postReceiptToCashbook()` and `bulkCashbook()`, and doing them together would have cost one visit rather than two, which is why it was asked now. Left open anyway: the existing toast already says "Review the category, then Post", so a prompt of a kind exists, and the VAT change is worth keeping small. When it is taken, it is one guard in the same place. |
| 54 | 16 step 10c, 17.5, 15 test 43 | **SUPERSEDED 2026-07-30. The change D precondition is gone with change D, amendment 68, and 10c is suspended for a different reason, amendment 70.** Original wording follows. **10c moves again: after the four IntelliBooks Desktop changes A to D, not immediately after test 41.** The ordering constraint it was placed under is unchanged, still before any console work. Test 43 moves to after the reset. | Paul's decision, 2026-07-29. Two reasons, and the second is the one that decides it. The Desktop app is about to be modified by the session briefed in `PROMPT_intellibooks_desktop_changes.md`, and resetting either side of a code change is two variables at once, which is the same reasoning that kept 10c out of the way of step 10. More concretely, **change D cannot be tested after the reset**: test 44 posts a receipt whose figures do not reconcile from the books to the cashbook, and the reset clears the books. Build D, test it against the test data that exists now, then clear down. Test 43 goes the other way and moves to **after** the reset, because 17.5 already requires a fresh fixture and one clean cycle to be confirmed post-reset, so a single Review item and a single OpenAI call serves both purposes instead of two. |
| 55 | 13A new, 16 steps 10a and 10b, 17.5 | **Decided: the client folder hierarchy is namespaced.** Everything this system owns moves under `Clients\{name}\_IntelliBooks\`, with `_Receipts\{tax year}\`, `_Review\`, `_Handover Pack\` and `_HMRC Summaries\` beneath it. The tax year folder keeps its bare `2026-27` form with **no** leading underscore. | Paul's decisions, 2026-07-29. A client folder is a mixture: `Clients\Paul Keating\` already holds `Document Requests`, `Misc` and eight loose PDFs from another tool alongside the two this system writes, and as engagement letters, correspondence and returns accumulate there is nothing to tell an operator which folders a program writes into. The leading underscore marks them as managed and sorts them to the top. `HMRC Summaries` replaces the proposed `HMRC_Mapping` because the folder holds period summary CSVs, one per period, and the mapping itself lives on `books.categories[].hmrc` and never goes there; naming it for the mapping would be the same fault as `Run Matching Analyser`. **The tax year exception is not a style preference.** Desktop's `listReceiptYears()` tests folder names against `/^\d{4}-\d{2}$/`, so `_2026-27` matches nothing, the year dropdown comes back empty and no filed receipt loads for any year. `determine_tax_year()` produces `2026-27` and both tools already agree on it. **Superseded in part 2026-09-02 by amendment 170: the parent folder survives as `Clients\{client_folder_name}\IntelliBooks\`, the leading underscores do not, and `_Review` is not a child at all.** |
| 56 | 13A new, 8.1 | **Locking the managed folders was asked and answered: it is not achievable on Windows with OneDrive, and detection replaces it.** | Paul asked on 2026-07-29 whether the folders could be writable only by the app. Three reasons no. NTFS permissions restrict by **user account**, not application, and both tools run as Paul, so any permission that admits them admits Explorer. Controlled Folder Access is genuinely per-application but its granularity collapses here: the pipeline is `python.exe`, so allowing it allows every Python script; Desktop is a browser using the File System Access API, so allowing it allows any site granted folder access; and `explorer.exe` needs allowing or ordinary file management breaks. And restrictive ACLs inside a OneDrive sync root are unwise: synced files do not reliably inherit the parent's permissions and OneDrive needs full access as the user to reconcile. **Prevention arrives free on AWS**, where an IAM policy can give write access to the app's role and nothing else, so no Windows workaround should be built. Detection is also strictly better than a lock would have been, because a lock stops only one of the ways these folders acquire files nobody intended: it would not stop a OneDrive conflict copy, and it would not have caught the 23 ghost receipts found on 2026-07-29. |
| 57 | 13A.4, and a change for the IntelliBooks session | **A live defect in Desktop: every auto-scanned filed receipt is added to the books twice.** `ingestReceiptFiles()` keys images with the extension stripped and then looks them up with it present, so a sidecar never finds its image. | Found 2026-07-29 while answering what happens to a manually added receipt, and confirmed across all four books files rather than inferred. **23 ghosts in total**, ids like `img_2026-07-24_PENNINE-CAFE-&-BAKERY_27.00`, gross 0, note "Image only. Edit details.": 11 of 23 receipts in `TEST`, 8 of 13 in `TEST2`, 4 of 13 in `PAUL`. **And the fingerprint that proves the mechanism rather than merely fitting it: in `TEST2` and `PAUL` the number of receipts carrying a thumbnail is exactly the number of ghosts**, 8 and 8, 4 and 4. The ghosts are the only receipts with images, because a sidecar-loaded receipt never finds its image. `TEST` has 9 further images because a receipt filed through `fileReviewReceipt()` is pushed to the books with the image it just read, without going through the scan at all, so the two code paths are visibly different in the data. Three consequences. The real receipt gets no thumbnail because the lookup returns null; `delete images[base]` removes nothing so the image falls through to the orphan loop and is pushed a second time; and the books file carries every image twice as base64, which is why `TEST-books.json` is 6.1 MB. Contained rather than harmless: `postReceiptToCashbook()` refuses a gross of 0, so a ghost cannot reach the cashbook until a human types an amount into it. Ordered ahead of the button rename and the delete guard because it is affecting books data while those are not. All affected data is test data that 10c clears, so no cleanup is needed if the fix lands first. **Built and verified 2026-07-29, with two corrections to the fix I specified, both found by the implementation session and both material.** First, the image must be claimed **before** the `isStatement` and already-in-books `continue` statements, not after them where the original code put it. A receipt already in the books leaves its image unclaimed and the image drops into the loose-image loop, so the twin still appears. That is every receipt filed through Review, because `fileReviewReceipt()` pushes to the books itself and the scan then hits the dedup `continue`. My specified fix would have passed a code review and failed its own manual check. It also explains the thumbnail arithmetic: a review-filed receipt gets a real entry with an image from the filing path **and** a ghost from the scan, which is why `TEST` has 20 thumbnails against 11 ghosts. Second, the loose-image loop must take its stem from `f.name` rather than the new lowercased lookup key, or `img_` ids gain a file extension, the dedup check misses, and every existing duplicate is added a third time. **And one thing I asserted that was not true:** I reported `if(imf&&/image/.test(imf.type\|\|"x")!==false)` as always true when `imf` is truthy. It is not. `.test()` returns a boolean and `!== false` on a boolean is the identity, so the expression is exactly `imf && /image/.test(...)`, and a PDF was already skipped. Redundant, not a defect, and the session checked rather than accepting it. |
| 58 | A change for the IntelliBooks session | **A receipt with no amount must be visible at a glance in the Desktop receipts list**, as a red pill reading "No amount" beside the supplier, keyed on `rGross(r)<=0`. | Paul asked on 2026-07-29 for the "Image only. Edit details." note to be a different colour. The note is the wrong thing to style: it renders as `<div class="muted small">`, deliberately dimmed, so the request means fighting the stylesheet, and it is a string written in one branch of one loop. The row already has a pill mechanism at lines 42 to 47, with a red `pill review` shown when `r.validation` is set and not `ok`; a loose image has empty validation and so gets nothing. A pill keyed on the amount is better on three counts: it survives the note wording changing, it sits in a column so a long list stays scannable, and it catches **every** receipt with no amount rather than only loose images, including one whose extraction found no gross and one an operator half-edited. Those cannot be posted either, since `postReceiptToCashbook()` refuses a gross of 0, and today they look fine. Related and flagged not fixed: the amber "To Cashbook" button is offered on rows the same function will refuse. |
| 59 | 57 | **Decided: the change E fix is forward-only and stays that way.** A receipt already in the books is skipped by the dedup `continue`, so it never acquires the thumbnail it should have had. 17 receipts are in that state, 12 in `PAUL` and 5 in `TEST2`. **Note the difference between a file count and a screen count**, which caught me out on 2026-07-29: the receipts list is filtered by the selected tax year, so `TEST2` shows **four** on 2026-27 and the fifth, a Canva receipt dated 2023-07-07, only appears on 2023-24. Three reasons. **The condition cannot recur after the reset**: a receipt filed through Desktop is pushed into the books with its image already in hand, and a receipt filed by the pipeline is loaded by the scan, which now pairs correctly on first sight. The 17 are historical artefacts of the defect rather than an ongoing gap. **The reset clears them anyway**, and delete-and-rescan repairs any one of them in the meantime, which is how the fix was verified. And **an "update the entry if it has no image" rule would fire on every scan for every PDF-backed receipt**, which correctly has no thumbnail and never will, so the rule would have to distinguish "missing" from "not applicable" for no benefit. Worth knowing before anyone concludes the fix has not worked on a client they have not touched: `TEST2` will show five thumbnail-less receipts indefinitely and that is expected. |
| 60 | A note for the IntelliBooks session | `runAnalyser()` keeps its name behind a button now labelled "Categorise from Rules". Left deliberately. | Flagged rather than fixed on 2026-07-29, correctly. Renaming the function means editing the `onclick` string too, which is behaviour-adjacent for no user-visible gain, and the name is not on screen. The mild irony is noted: a misleading name is what change B existed to fix. The difference is that a label lies to an operator and a function name only misleads a reader. Fold the rename in whenever someone is next editing that region for another reason; do not open the file for it. |
| 61 | 17.4, a question for Paul | **Changing one transaction's category from its row dropdown silently creates or overwrites a statement rule. The bulk path asks first; the single-row path does not.** | Found by Paul on 2026-07-29 while testing change B, and confirmed in the source. The row dropdown calls `setCategory(id, cat, true)`, and with `learn` true it derives a key from the description and runs `if(ex) ex.category=cat; else books.rules.push({pattern:key,category:cat})`. No modal, no toast, no confirmation. `bulkCategorise()` does the same job and behaves quite differently: it builds `pendingRules`, opens "Learn rules from this?", lets the operator edit each pattern or untick it, and shows a `pill review` reading "updates rule (was X)" precisely when an existing rule would change. So the two paths to the same outcome have opposite safety characteristics, and the unguarded one is the one used constantly. **Learning itself is intended**, per change log item 2, so this is not a defect report. The sharp edge is the silent **overwrite**: correcting a single transaction can reverse a rule set deliberately earlier and change how every future transaction from that supplier is categorised, with nothing on screen to say so. Note the contrast with 11.3 on the pipeline side, which forbids automatic learning outright because one correction against a misread supplier name poisons the mapping table and the engine then applies the wrong code confidently. Desktop is a different system with a human at the keyboard, so the same rule need not apply, but the asymmetry between its own two paths is hard to defend. ~~**Paul's decision:** leave it, warn on overwrite only, or route the row dropdown through the same preview.~~ **Decided 2026-07-29: warn on overwrite only**, built as change G. Creating a rule from a first-time categorisation is the intended behaviour and asking would be noise; silently reversing a rule set deliberately is the only part that can cost anything. The warning names the old category and the new one, because "a rule changed" without saying which way is not actionable. |
| 62 | 17.4, a question for Paul | **Two statement rules can exist for one supplier with no visible relationship, and the amount-conditioned one silently wins.** | Flagged by me at amendment 61 and returned in concrete form by the implementation session on 2026-07-29, confirmed against `bestRuleFor()`: `const pool=conditioned.length?conditioned:cands` means an amount-conditioned rule beats the pattern-only rule for any amount satisfying its condition, and among the pool the longest pattern wins. Two consequences, both of which will be reported as "the app ignored me" rather than as a rule problem. **First**, where an amount rule already exists for a supplier, a category set from the row dropdown updates the pattern-only rule, the change G toast confirms a rule changed, and future transactions at that amount are still categorised the old way. The operator has been told the opposite of what happens. **Second**, where *only* an amount rule exists, the dropdown creates a pattern-only rule that can never fire for those amounts. Silent creation is by design, per amendment 61, but this particular rule is born shadowed. Neither is a defect in change G and neither is in its scope. The underlying question is whether two rules for one supplier should be visible as related in the rules table, and whether the narrower one winning should be stated somewhere. Recorded in change log item 28. **Decided 2026-07-29: change H, the toast names the other rules. The rules table is deferred with a self-measuring trigger.** Change H extends `setCategory()` so that on **both** the create and the update path it reports any other rule sharing the pattern, which collapses both consequences into one condition and one sentence. **Corrected in the same conversation:** I argued the table work could wait partly because no pattern currently has both kinds of rule. Paul rejected that reasoning and was right, since six rules across a handful of test transactions says nothing about the rate in a real practice with thousands. The deferral survives on grounds that do not depend on frequency: the reset clears every rule, so nothing built now would run against real data; the toast catches the operator who is **not** looking, which a table only helps someone who is; and the console work from step 12 introduces GL codes and may change how rules are presented, so a table redesign now risks being done twice. **The trigger is change H itself.** The first time that toast mentions an amount rule, the situation is live and the table work is due. I considered adding it as a reconciliation finding instead and rejected that: finding 8 reads only the filenames in `IntelliBooks\Books\`, whereas counting rules means the pipeline parsing the internal shape of Desktop's books, which is a coupling worth avoiding for a statistic. |
| 63 | A correction to change C | **Adding or deleting a category never refreshes the statement rules table, so its "Categorise as" dropdown is stale in both directions.** | Found by Paul on 2026-07-29 running change C's own manual check, which could not be completed because a newly added category was not offered in the rules dropdown. Confirmed in the source. `addCategory()` ends `scheduleSave();renderCats();renderBank();` and never calls `renderRules()`; `delCategory()`, including the version just written for change C, ends identically. So a new category cannot be pointed at by a rule until something else redraws that table, and **a deleted category carries on being offered there**, which is the more serious direction because selecting it writes a rule referencing a category that no longer exists, which is precisely what change C exists to prevent. The receipts dropdown appears to work only because the Edit modal is rebuilt from `books.categories` each time it opens; the bank dropdowns work because `renderBank()` is called. `renderAll()` already exists at line 2324 and calls all seven renderers. **This is a pre-existing defect in `addCategory()` that change C inherited by copying its ending**, and it means change C is not complete: its guard is right and its reporting is right, but the table it guards can still offer a deleted name. Notable as a process point too: it was found by an operator following a manual check, not by reading the code, and neither the implementation session nor I spotted it while looking straight at both functions. |

H and F are not.** The boundary is: A, B, C, E, G, H, F built and tested; D, 10a, 10b, 10c specified and not started. ~~**Amended 2026-07-30 by amendment 66: change I joins D as outstanding, and the two are to be built in the same visit.**~~ **Overtaken later the same day: change D is cancelled, amendment 68, and change I was built and its check passed. Identical duplicates are harmless while they stay identical, because `bestRuleFor()`'s tie-break is `r.pattern.length>b.pattern.length`, a strict comparison, so the first survives and both carry the same category. **They do not have to stay identical.** `setCategory()`'s learn path does `books.rules.find(r=>r.pattern===key&&!r.op)`, and `find` returns the first match only, so a category set from the row dropdown updates one duplicate and leaves the other holding the old category. And **the comparison has to be on the normalised pattern**, which is what makes this more than tidiness: `ruleMatchForm()` strips everything outside `A-Z` and a space and removes noise words including `LTD` and `CARD PAYMENT`, so `Apple Bill Ltd`, `apple-bill` and `CARD PAYMENT APPLE BILL` all reduce to `APPLE BILL`. Change D was built to warn when a receipt's `net + VAT` did not equal its gross. **That test is not a validity test, it is a single-rate test.** Of the six receipts in the database that fail it, five fail because the figures do not account for the whole gross, which is what an ordinary mixed-rate receipt looks like: an optician selling exempt dispensing beside standard-rated frames, a café bill with a cold item on it. Only the direction `net + VAT > gross` is impossible, because the parts cannot exceed the whole. Built as specified, the warning would have fired on correct receipts until it meant nothing, which is worse than no warning because it trains the operator to dismiss it. **And the same test lives in the pipeline** at `worker/validation/rules.py`, so those five correct receipts were routed to `needs_review`. Section 18 replaces the warning with a treatment of the underlying question: what the transaction records, what the receipt is for, and where a check earns its place. |

And it is indistinguishable at a glance from the defect amendment 49 fixed, so **a duplicate-`client_id` check added to guard against that would break multi-address clients**; the test is whether the other columns match. Entity recognition from receipt content is also not ruled out, since `categorisations_client_rules` is already the right table shape and `client_id=UNKNOWN` is already the "not yet resolved" state. A cross-entity receipt split would need a join table, and Paul's own point is that the accounting usually wants a recharge rather than a split, because the supply was made to one entity. |

### v1.9, 2026-08-17

| # | Section | Change | Why |
|---|---|---|---|
| 96 | 5, 5.5, 11.1, 12.3, 13, 13.1, 16 step 12, 18.10 | **The chart of accounts leaves both systems. It is not a table in `receipts.db` and it is not part of the console.** It lives at `C:\Users\PDK7\OneDrive - Intellitax Accounting Limited\IntelliCharts\`, as `COA_MASTER_v1.csv`, **122 accounts in 20 columns**, with `build_coa.py` generating every other file and running every check. Working name **IntelliCharts**; it is a folder, a CSV and a script, not software. **Ten decisions of 5 to 17 August, none of which any repository file held until now.** Four-digit codes on Sage 50's published ranges. A separate `vat_recoverability` column. KashFlow dropped. Sage Accounting deferred with no import file generated. Sage Final Accounts as a mapping table rather than columns. `coa_destination_exceptions.csv` so an exception is data with a reason. A drift check that stops the build. Two ideas dropped as over-engineering. And the `status` filter goes into `catOptions()` in the same Desktop change. **18.10's chart of accounts item is closed: `chart_of_accounts_DRAFT.csv` is not extended and IntelliBooks becomes a consumer of the master on the same footing as Xero.** | Paul's decisions, 5 to 17 August, taken in a separate advisory chat that wrote nothing into the repository. **Three reasons for the move, and the third is the one that settles it:** not every client goes on IntelliBooks or Intellibills, some go on Xero, FreeAgent or QuickBooks; the module will be called from **Sodium Practice Management**; and a thing whose whole purpose is to be ledger-agnostic does not belong inside one ledger's companion. **13.1 survives intact.** The four levels, the copy rather than the fall-back, the parent-selection rule, `provenance` and the four import dispositions all stand. **Only the housing changed, and 13.1 now describes how a chart is issued to a client rather than where the master is kept.** **On four-digit codes:** Sage 50 is the only destination where the code decides which report an account lands on, and the others treat it as a label, so its ranges win. A side benefit worth keeping: **any three-digit code appearing after this is provably legacy**, which is what the 100 vendor mappings in `categorisations_client_vendors` still carry. **On `vat_recoverability`:** it exists because 18.4's six rate values cannot express "standard rated but the input tax is blocked", which is cars, business entertainment and non-business use. Rate and recoverability are two facts and one field could not hold both. **On Sage Final Accounts:** Sage ships a different standard chart per client type and its own two share 176 codes of which **85 mean something different**, so one column would have been wrong about half the time. `3210` is Capital withdrawn for a sole trader and a revaluation reserve movement for a company, and Drawings is `3260`. That became a 237-row table. **On Sage Accounting:** its CONTROL ACCOUNT column is required-if-populated, Sage does not publish the permitted values, and **Sage's own two pages contradict each other on whether a control account can be renamed on import.** Building a wrong file was judged worse than waiting, so it is built by hand from the first real Sage Accounting client. Confirmed 2026-08-17 by reading `build_coa.py`: no such file is written, and the 5 August note is stale on this point. **On the industry level, a fact that makes 13.1 cheaper than it looks:** `categorisations_firm_vendors` already keys on `business_type` and has no `firm_id` column, so despite its name it is already the industry tier. **Section column corrected 2026-08-17 after `89e0603`, which added `5`, `11.1` and `12.3`.** All three were edited by this amendment and cited it by name, and none was listed. **Found by the implementation session mapping all 33 changed lines to their enclosing heading, rather than checking the two the commit message named.** The same run caught the message itself saying "two more found by enumerating" when the enumeration had returned three, the third being `11.1`, where `coa_accounts` was named as the source the console reads code options from. **The enumeration was mine and it was right; the summary of it was written from memory a few hours later and was not.** That is amendment 97's fault in the same commit as amendment 97. **And the reason there are two tax columns rather than one, which was in the 5 August note and in no repository file until now: SA103F and MTD ITSA are different schemes and neither is a subset of the other.** SA103F identifies by numbered box, 15 to 30, being 2 income and **14** expense boxes. **MTD has no numbers at all**: its categories are names, set by a direction under regulation 10 of the Income Tax (Digital Obligations) Regulations 2026, and there are 15, being 2 income and **13** expense. Three differences decide the design. **SA103F box 24 is one box, "Advertising and business entertainment costs", and MTD splits it into "Advertising" and "Business entertainment costs"**, which is why `8200 Advertising and marketing` and `7405 Business entertainment` are separate accounts; the VAT rule pointed the same way, since entertainment's input tax is blocked and advertising's is not. **Box 27 irrecoverable debts and box 29 depreciation and loss or profit on sale of assets are not MTD categories at all**, so `8100`, `8000`, `8001` and `4200` carry an SA103F box and a deliberately blank MTD category. **And a property client needs SA105, a third set of boxes, which is an industry chart under 13.1 rather than a variation of the master.** Verified 2026-08-17 against the HMRC direction as updated 27 March 2026 and against `SA103F_2026.pdf`, which **closes the 5 August note's own open item**: it graded the absence of those two categories medium-high and asked for a second pair of eyes before it was relied on. It is now high. **One consequence worth stating because it decides how the sixteenth box is briefed: depreciation is never reported quarterly.** It has no MTD category, so it appears only at the year end and only under traditional accounting. |
| 97 | 5.5, 15, 18.10, `CLAUDE.md` | **Three verification findings from taking the workstream over, and one is a defect that adopting the master would make reachable for the first time.** **`COA_MASTER_v1.csv` account `4200 Profit or loss on sale of assets` has `type` `income` and `sa103f_box` `29`, which is an expense box. It is the only account in the master where the type and the box point in opposite directions**, and `exportHMRC()` at line 2107 of `IntelliBooks-Desktop-v3.html` reads `totals[m.box]+=(m.type==="income")?t.amount:-t.amount`, so a profit on disposal **increases** box 29 and total expenses when it should reduce them. **Open, and Paul's to settle.** **Second: the contiguity check this document has relied on all along was giving right answers for the wrong reason.** **Third: the SA103F count in the Desktop brief is loose.** SA103F 2026 has **16 mappable boxes of which 14 are expense boxes**, being 17 to 30, not "16 expense boxes". | Found 2026-08-17 while verifying the handover rather than from any report. **On 4200 the accounting is right and the code's assumption is wrong:** box 29 is `Depreciation and loss or profit on sale of assets` and it genuinely holds both, so it can fall as well as rise, while line 2107 infers direction from the account's type and every other account has the two agreeing. **Adding the depreciation key is what makes it reachable**, because until now 4200 fell to `other`, which is box 30 and allowable, and was wrong in a different way. Three master accounts carry box 29: `4200`, `8000 Depreciation charge` and `8001 Amortisation charge`. **On the contiguity check**, flagged by the implementation session disclosing that its own first regex swept up an unrelated table: mine did the same and never showed it. It matched **103 numbered rows across the whole file** and reported "contiguous 1 to 95, leftovers `[]`", because it compared with a **set** difference and section 13A's findings table is numbered 1 to 8, a subset of 1 to 95. **By count there were eight leftovers and it printed none.** Had that table been numbered 96 to 103 the longest-run logic would have merged it and reported 103 amendments as contiguous. **The corrected method, now in `CLAUDE.md`: bound the scope to the amendment record's own line boundaries, then assert the list equals `range(first, last+1)` and check duplicates explicitly.** Re-run scoped: 95 rows, strictly ascending, no duplicates. **The lesson is amendment 94's again in a new costume:** the answer was right, so nothing prompted a second look, and a check that cannot fail is not a check. **On SA103F**, read from `SA103F_2026.pdf` at HMRC directly rather than from the brief: business expenses are boxes 17 to 30, fourteen of them, and the sixteen is 2 income boxes plus those 14, which is exactly the sixteen distinct values in the master's `sa103f_box` column. The substance of the brief stands, only its arithmetic is wrong. |
| 98 | 13, 96, and the IntelliBooks Desktop brief | **The `hmrc` key on a books category is renamed. The name is ambiguous and must not survive the adoption change.** Paul's decision, 2026-08-17. `books.categories[].hmrc` holds an **SA103F box** and nothing else, but its name reads as though it covers HMRC generally. **Recommended name `sa103fBox`**, matching the master's `sa103f_box`, in camelCase to match `transferId` and `clientCode` elsewhere in the books file. The operator-facing label "HMRC box", in `renderCats()` and in the handover pack's `categories.csv` and `transactions.csv`, changes with it. | **Amendment 96 is the reason it cannot stay.** SA103F, MTD ITSA and SA105 are three different schemes: MTD has no box numbers and no category for boxes 27 or 29, and a property client needs SA105 entirely. A field called `hmrc` invites a future session to put an MTD category or a CT figure in it, and **nothing in the code would object**, because the only validation is that the value is one of sixteen strings. **It is also wrong on screen for the client it most affects:** `renderCats()` shows the dropdown for every client regardless of entity type, so a limited company gets an SA103F box selector that means nothing for it. **The window is now.** The field is about to be written across 122 accounts in every books file by the adoption change, and every existing books entry is test data with zero transactions, so renaming costs a find and replace today and a migration afterwards. **Blast radius, enumerated: one field name in the books file, five consumers in `IntelliBooks-Desktop-v3.html`** at the migration default, the two handover pack writers, `exportHMRC()`'s lookup and `renderCats()`'s dropdown, **plus `addCategory()` which sets it, `intellibooks_seed.js` which writes it on 75 rows, and the `hmrc` column heading in `intellibooks_seed.csv`.** Nothing in Intellibills, the database or any import file is affected, because none of them carries the field. ~~One adjacent question is deliberately left open here: whether the field should hold the box number rather than a string key.~~ **Closed the same day by Paul: it holds the number, and IntelliBooks shows the number and the description together.** So `sa103fBox` carries `"29"`, matching the master's `sa103f_box` exactly, and the seed becomes a straight copy with **no translation table at all**. `HMRC_BOXES` stops being key-and-label pairs and becomes **number-and-label** pairs, in SA103F order 15 to 30. **Keep it as a string rather than an integer**, so it matches the master byte for byte and there is no place where `15` and `"15"` can fail to compare. **The gain that was not the point and is worth more than the rename:** `exportHMRC()`'s CSV currently writes rows like `travel,"Car, van and travel expenses",1234.56`, which requires the reader to know what `travel` means. It will write `20,"Car, van and travel expenses",1234.56`, **so the file finally carries the actual box numbers a person or a bridging tool needs.** It also removes the two hardcoded key names at line 2112, where total income reads `totals.turnover + totals.other_income` and becomes boxes 15 and 16, and the expense total becomes every box that is not one of those two. |
| 99 | 13.1, 98, and the IntelliBooks Desktop brief | **`client_type` is added to the client record, with three values: `sole_trader`, `partnership`, `company`. LLP is deliberately excluded for now.** It is what makes the master's `applies_to` column usable: a client receives every account whose `applies_to` is `all` or matches its own type, so a company gets 120 accounts and an unincorporated client 117. **And a partnership needs one capital and one drawings account per partner**, so two blocks are reserved: `3200-3209` capital introduced and `3210-3219` drawings, generated from a `partners` list on the client record. The master continues to ship only `3200` and `3210`, which is exactly right for a sole trader. | Paul's decisions, 2026-08-17. **`applies_to` has existed since 5 August with nothing able to read it, because no client record anywhere says whether a client is a company.** Three values rather than two, because a sole trader and a partnership are both `unincorporated` for the purpose of which accounts are relevant, and differ in how many of them are created. **Why separate accounts per partner rather than one account and an analysis tag:** each partner's capital balance determines what that partner is owed and feeds their own return, so it must be separately reportable, and IntelliBooks has no concept of a tag or a grouping report. Codes were available: Sage 50 treats `3100-3299` as Capital and Reserves, Reserves, and the master uses only `3100`, `3200` and `3210` in that whole band, so both blocks land under the correct Sage 50 heading with no extra mapping. **Three rules that stop it rotting.** A departing partner's code is never reused, because the account keeps its balance and history. Ten partners per block is the ceiling and an eleventh spills into `3220`, still Reserves, so it degrades quietly rather than breaking. And a partnership with no partners entered gets no capital or drawings accounts at all, rather than one belonging to nobody, which makes the partner list an ordering requirement on the create-client screen. **A `partners` array is not a new shape:** the client record already carries `phv`. |
| 100 | 96, 98, 8.6, and the IntelliBooks Desktop brief | **The period selector offers MTD quarters and the only report that consumes them is in SA103F boxes, which is the wrong shape for a quarter.** `hmrcPeriods()` at line 2058 of `IntelliBooks-Desktop-v3.html` builds four quarterly periods when a client is flagged for MTD, and puts them in the dropdown beside the Export CSV button on the Reports tab. `exportHMRC()` reports whatever period is chosen against the 16 SA103F boxes. **An MTD quarterly update needs the 15 MTD categories instead**, which are a different list: no box numbers at all, advertising and business entertainment split into two, and no category for irrecoverable debts or for depreciation. **And the list of boxes is renamed from `HMRC_BOXES` to `SA103F_BOXES`**, for the same reason the field became `sa103fBox`. | Paul's decisions, 2026-08-17, while checking the terminology for exactly this kind of ambiguity. **The naming and the crossing are two different problems and only one is cosmetic.** Renaming the list is a find and replace in five places. **The period selector is the two schemes actually meeting in the code rather than in a name**, and it produces a correct total in a shape nobody can file: select a quarter and you get a quarterly figure broken into self assessment boxes. **Harmless today because `exportHMRC()` has never been run**, and it becomes wrong the first time an MTD client's quarter is exported. **Not fixed, and the reason is that the fix is a second report rather than a correction:** a quarterly update needs its own output shaped to the MTD categories, and the master already carries `mtd_itsa_category` on 67 accounts ready for it, read by nothing. **Four operator-facing names carry the same ambiguity and are deliberately left:** `exportHMRC()`, the card headed "HMRC Summary Export", the "HMRC box" column in the handover pack, and the folder `Clients\{name}\HMRC Summaries\`. The folder is the one that must not be renamed casually, because a client may already have been shown it. |
### v1.10, 2026-08-18

| # | Section | Change | Why |
|---|---|---|---|
| 101 | 11.1, 96, 16 step 12 | **The console's GL code list has no implemented reader for the master, and the fallback it has been standing on is now empty.** `list_gl_code_options_from_vendors()` at `worker/database/repository.py:177` UNIONs `categorisations_client_vendors` and `categorisations_firm_vendors` and feeds `ResolutionView.gl_code_options` at `worker/resolution/service.py:417`. **That is 11.1's fallback, not its primary source.** Amendment 96 moved the primary source to `IntelliCharts\COA_MASTER_v1.csv` and **nothing reads it from there**. So when the 100 legacy rows were dropped from `categorisations_client_vendors` on 2026-08-18, and `categorisations_firm_vendors` was already empty, that method began returning an empty list. **Not fixed, and the reason is that the fix is a CSV reader rather than a correction.** | Found 2026-08-18 by the implementation session while establishing what read the table before deleting from it. **It stopped and asked rather than proceeding, which is why this is a finding and not an incident.** **The delete did not create the gap; it made it impossible to miss.** Before it the console had a source that was wrong but populated: 23 stale three-digit codes pointing at accounts that no longer exist. **A wrong but populated dropdown is worse than an empty one, because it can be used.** **Two things keep this off the critical path, and both were measured rather than assumed.** The console does not exist: `Flask` appears zero times in `requirements.txt` and in `requirements-dev.txt`, `worker\` has no templates, static or web layer, and `app.py` has zero occurrences of `Flask` or `render_template`, so `ResolutionView.gl_code_options` is a field nothing renders. And **11.1 already specifies the behaviour for an empty source**: free-text entry for a code not yet in the chart, recorded in the notes so it can be promoted later, plus a banner saying the chart has not been loaded. So the console stays usable when it is built and simply offers no picklist until the reader exists. **What must not happen is a session reseeding the vendor tables to refill the dropdown**, which would put three-digit codes back and undo exactly the property the four-digit scheme was chosen for. **The categorisation engine's read path is unaffected and was checked rather than assumed:** its exact-match layer receives `None` and falls through, and its fuzzy layer is guarded on a non-empty candidate list, so an empty table is "no match" and not an error. |
| 102 | 15, 97, and the IntelliBooks Desktop change log | **Amendment 97's `4200` finding is closed.** It was fixed on 2026-08-17 inside the chart adoption change, and **the fix takes the direction of a figure from the SA103F box rather than from the account's type.** `exportHMRC()` now reads `totals[m.box]+=INCOME_BOXES.includes(m.box)?t.amount:-t.amount`. Boxes 15 and 16 are income boxes, 17 to 30 are expense boxes, and that is where the sign comes from. Recorded in change log item 36 of `IntelliBooks\App\Docs\IntelliBooks-Change-Log.md`. **Amendment 97 records it as "Open, and Paul's to settle" and that wording is now stale.** | Paul's decision, and it is the general statement rather than a patch. `4200 Profit or loss on sale of assets` is typed `income` and points at box 29, an expense box, so inferring direction from the type made a profit on disposal **increase** box 29 when it must reduce it: depreciation of 1,000 with a 300 profit on sale gave 1,300 instead of 700, **and both figures look plausible**, which is what made it worth fixing rather than noting. **Three alternatives were rejected and the reasons are worth keeping.** Special-casing box 29 would have put a hardcoded exception in the code. Splitting `4200` into separate profit and loss accounts does not fix the sign at all. Reclassifying `4200` as an expense, which TaxCalc's own chart does, would leave that row contradicting its five other classification columns. **The chosen fix also repairs any future account of the same shape**, and the totalling line further down `exportHMRC()` already worked that way, so the change made the function internally consistent as well as correct. **Verified 2026-08-18 by the consultant session by reading the function back rather than from the change log:** the box-driven line is present and the comment above it records the worked example. |
### v1.11, 2026-08-18

**Misdated at the time: these six were written on 2026-08-20, and amendment 109 records it.** The heading keeps the date the header carried, so the trail matches amendment 109's finding rather than quietly resolving it.

| # | Section | Change | Why |
|---|---|---|---|
| 103 | all of section 18, 11.1, 13.1, and a new document | **Two products, and the boundary between them. Recorded in full in `2026-08-18_BOUNDARY_two_products.md`, which is above this document on the question of which product a thing belongs to.** Intellibills, the receipts capture app, is sellable standalone to a firm that keeps its books elsewhere. IntelliBooks, the bookkeeping app, is Intellibills plus the books and is **never sold on its own**. **The boundary: Intellibills owns the document and everything read from it; IntelliBooks owns the books.** The test for anything new is whether it is a document function or a books function. **The rule: no function may live only in the other product.** Take IntelliBooks away, and Intellibills must still work. The dependency runs one way only: IntelliBooks may depend on Intellibills for anything, Intellibills on IntelliBooks for nothing. **And nothing in Intellibills may be named after IntelliBooks:** Intellibills publishes to the destination configured for a client and does not know what is at the other end. | Paul's decisions, 2026-08-18, and the reason is that he may sell either product. **An earlier form of the boundary said it was the publish step, and it was wrong**: it equated "after publish" with "bookkeeping", and those are not the same. Recorded as rejected so nobody reinstates it. **Three breaches were found the same day and none was a decision to place an Intellibills function inside IntelliBooks.** Add Receipts and the Review queue are there because IntelliBooks had a screen and Intellibills did not. The copy into `Clients\` is there because Post was the sensible moment and Post exists only in one product. **Each was a reasonable decision about something else whose side effect was a breach**, which is why the rule has to be applied deliberately rather than trusted to be obvious. **The one that proved the point:** filing a document into the firm's client folder involves no transaction, ledger, category or VAT, so it is a document function, yet it could only happen at Post. It was found not by inspection but by Paul asking what happens in the standalone version. **The Receipts tab is Intellibills' front end hosted in IntelliBooks' shell; the conflation is in the hosting, not the functions, and hosting is not ownership.** Five things on it depend on the books: attach to a transaction, post to cashbook, Post Selected and the attached marker with its default filter are omitted standalone, and the category dropdown is re-sourced from `IntelliCharts` rather than the books file. **The tab keeps the name Receipts**; renaming it to Intellibills was decided and withdrawn the same day. |
| 104 | 18.3, 18.2a, and the interim in section 0.5 of `2026-07-31_PLAN_reset_and_restructure.md` | **The publish step is specified. Per client, one publishing destination, held as a column on the client record. Per destination, its address held separately as an Intellibills setting.** Two levels, not one: which destination a client uses is a client fact; where that destination is reached is firm configuration. An always-on CSV export is a separate switch and not a second destination. **The inbox is one folder, not one per client, and the client identity travels inside the item.** An item is **one JSON file per receipt with the image embedded as base64**, written by temp-name-and-rename so a half-written file can never be read. **IntelliBooks drains automatically when a client is opened, and a visible manual control with a waiting count allows a retry.** **Scheduled 2026-08-21 by amendment 137 as sub-steps 10f.1 to 10f.10. Until then step 10f named this amendment and described none of its ten parts.** | Paul's decisions, 2026-08-18. **One folder rather than one per client, and this reversed a proposal made the same day**: per-client folders make the sender know three things about the receiver, being where the inbox is, that it is subdivided, and that the subdivision key is the client. Only the first is legitimate. In the cloud this is a POST to one endpoint with the client in the payload, and the receiver decides where it goes; per-client folders would leak the receiver's internal organisation into the sender. **The single folder also turns out to be better on its own terms**, because everything waiting for every client is visible in one view rather than hidden behind a client selection. **One file rather than an image plus a sidecar, and the reason is amendment 57**: pairing by filename produced 23 ghost receipts and a 6.1 MB books file, and a single file cannot half-arrive with its data missing. It is also already the shape of an API call, so when IntelliBooks becomes a service the file body becomes the request body and only the transport changes. Base64 costs about a third in size, accepted as temporary and gone on the move to the cloud. **The drain trigger matches what `scanFiledReceipts()` does today**, so it needs no new habit, and the manual control exists because a drain that fails halfway currently has no retry short of switching client and back. **One consequence for the acceptance test in section 0.5.1:** its fifth check requires the inbox to be empty after a drain, and with one folder that must read *empty of that client's items*. |
| 105 | 12.4, 13.1, 18.2c, 44, and `clients.csv` | **One client registry, owned by Intellibills, and four fields where there were seven overlapping ones.** IntelliBooks stops holding its own client list; book-only attributes are added to the Intellibills record. **`client_id`**, system generated and unchangeable, for every database row, every message between products, the books filename, the two modules' own folders, logs, backups and exports. **`client_name`**, display only, freely editable, and **never used to build a path**. **`client_folder_name`**, which names the client folder in the firm's filing structure, prefilled from the name, editable, then fixed once a folder exists. **`capture_token`**, random, per client and **revocable**, whose only job is the capture link. **There is no `client_code`.** `business_type` becomes `trade` and `clientType` becomes `entity_type`. **Firm level is implemented now, not later**: `firms.csv` real, `firm_id` carried throughout, two firms possible. | Paul's decisions, 2026-08-18, after an audit of every client identity field. **The word "code" was doing four jobs and none of them well.** The audit found the client code in nine places, the name in four, and two internal keys with the same role in two systems that never reconcile. **On 2026-08-18 `clients.csv` and `IntelliBooks-Practice.json` had no client in common at all**, with nothing checking and nothing reporting, because the two codes were typed independently in two places. **Two registries with a shared key is the worst of three options**, keeping the duplication and adding an obligation to keep them in step. **A separate `client_code` could not be justified** once it was accepted that both it and `client_id` would be permanent: two permanent identifiers for one thing is duplication, and readability alone does not earn a field. **`capture_token` is a credential, not an identifier**, which is why it survives: it can be reissued when a link leaks, where today one shared `uploadKey` serves every client and cannot be revoked for one. **`business_type` and `clientType` are not two spellings of one fact** but the trade and the legal form, two axes whose similar names invite one to be read as the other. **And a new defect found by the audit:** the two sides normalise the client name differently, the pipeline collapsing runs of whitespace where IntelliBooks does not, so a client named with a double space produces two different folders with no error. `client_folder_name` removes it, along with both normalisers, which exist only because a label was pressed into service as a path. |
| 106 | 18.2b, 18.9, and 103 | **The copy into `Clients\` becomes Intellibills' function with two triggers, and it is the only writer.** Per firm: on successful publish, at Post, or never. **Standalone Intellibills can only offer the first**, which is why the function had to move. **`fileReviewReceipt()` stops writing into `Clients\` at all**: a completed review item reaches the client folder by the same route as any other receipt, so there is one writer at one moment. **The Review queue stops touching the pipeline's folders**: review items are published to the inbox marked as needing review, and completion or discard goes back as a resolution note. **And the Post-time write and the closure of the 18.3 handoff land together**, not separately. **Scheduled 2026-08-21 by amendment 137 as sub-steps 10f.11, 10f.12, 10f.14, 10f.15 and 10f.16. Until then step 10f named this amendment and described none of its five parts.** | Paul's decisions, 2026-08-18. **The trigger is what broke it.** 18.2b chose Post because a folder fed straight from capture shows the client duplicates and misfires, and that reasoning stands. But "accepted" had a second meaning nobody noticed: **validated and published successfully**, which gates exactly the same rubbish, because nothing that failed validation, needed review or was flagged a possible duplicate ever publishes. **Two triggers rather than one because Paul asked for both**, and the combined product can offer either. **On the Review queue:** it reads, deletes and writes across the module boundary in three separate filesystem operations, none of which a cloud IntelliBooks could perform. It stays on screen where it is; what changes is the mechanism, and the resolution-note half already exists and already works. **On landing together:** closing the handoff stops anything reaching client folders, and if the write is not there the folders go quiet with nothing reporting it. They are also cheaper together, being the same moment in the same code, and 0.5.1's first check tests one against the presence of the other. **Section 18.9's cancellation table gains three rows it was missing**, two of which are these. |
| 107 | 18.5, and the open items in `IntelliBooks-Change-Log.md` | **Semantic duplicates are handled, and the policy is decided rather than parked.** Four parts. **One: duplicates are detected on the extracted values, not only on the file hash** — same client, same supplier, same document date, same gross — marked `possible_duplicate`, routed to Review, and never published. **The words "same client" were dropped when step 10f was written and are scheduled at sub-step 10f.19 by amendment 137. This decision was never wrong; it was never carried.** **Two: the client folder handles its own name collision** rather than renaming through it. Identical bytes: skip. Different bytes: keep the suffix and flag. **Three: possible duplicates are marked as such on the Receipts tab.** **Four: deleting a possible duplicate removes its copy from the client folder**, performed by Intellibills' own front end, and every write and delete is logged. **The route is Review and stop, not warn and proceed.** | Paul's decisions, 2026-08-18. The change log had this parked for a decision after the pilot: two photographs of one physical receipt have different hashes, so the check misses them and both are filed with `-2` and `-3` suffixes. **Detection on values catches every arrival route**, including the case image comparison cannot see, where the same receipt arrives once as a phone photo and once as an emailed PDF. **The folder check is nearly free** because the filename is already `{date}_{supplier}_{gross}` so a semantic duplicate already collides; only the response was wrong. **Review and stop was chosen deliberately, and the consultant session had wrongly reached for 18.4's "the system alerts, it never prevents" to justify warn-and-proceed.** That rule is about VAT and about nothing else. **And there is no warning mechanism to hang one on:** the word duplicate appears four times in `IntelliBooks-Desktop-v3.html` and not once about receipts, and `postWarnings()` fires at one moment only, posting a receipt to the cashbook. Inventing a warning pattern now would be work with no evidence behind it. **On the delete: unposting relates to a transaction and deleting a duplicate relates to a document**, so 18.2b's rule that a copy is never withdrawn still stands, because it concerns the books changing rather than the document having been wrong to be there. **The log is not only an audit trail**: two colliding receipts produce a file and a `-2`, and nothing in the receipt record says which is which, so without the log the delete is guesswork. **The population is small:** a detected duplicate never publishes and an identical file is skipped before a second copy exists, so this path is only ever reached when two receipts both got through and a human then judged one to be a duplicate. |
| 108 | 8.6, and `IntelliBooks-Desktop-v3.html` | **Settings are given a structure, and one tab stops calling itself Settings.** **Two levels: Firm Settings and Client Settings.** Within each, two sub-headings: **Intellibills Settings** and **IntelliBooks Settings**. **There is no system level on any page.** **Bank Accounts, Categories and Learned Statement Rules are not settings** and the tab holding them is renamed **Client Data**, with nothing on it moving or removed. **Firm Settings becomes a new menu item with an empty page.** The top menu takes three groups: New Client and the client dropdown left; Bank Transactions, Receipts, Reports and Client Data centre; Clients and Firm Settings right. **The firm's top-level client folder path is a setting and applies in the local app.** | Paul's decisions, 2026-08-18. Settings were in five places at once and nobody had a list: `.env`, `config.py`, `IntelliBooks-Practice.json`, Netlify environment variables, and constants in code. **There is no system level because everything above firm level turned out to be one of two things**, neither a setting in the sense of something a user changes: an engineering constant nobody should touch, being the extraction engine, the model, the validation tolerance, the poll interval and internal naming; or a secret belonging in environment configuration. A system settings page would have been empty or a page of things not to change. **On the renamed tab:** a category is an account you post to, a learned rule is accumulated knowledge, and a bank account is a real thing with a balance. None is a preference set once. They were filed under Settings because that tab became the home for anything that was not a transaction. **The Firm Settings page is deliberately empty**, because the list of what belongs on it has not been produced and building it first would mean building it twice. **Two decisions taken earlier the same day were withdrawn:** renaming the Receipts tab to Intellibills, and putting an "Intellibills Settings" button on it. **Where Client Settings live in the menu is deliberately left open** until that list exists; today they are reachable only through Clients then Edit, which Paul has recorded as unintuitive. |

| 115 | 16 step 10d, `worker/database/repository.py`, `worker/database/schema.py` | **Three things, two scheduled into step 10d and one named and deliberately not scheduled.** **One: a receipt written with `client_id = UNKNOWN` is a review item and reports, and is never `status = ok`.** The value stays; what goes is `UNKNOWN` arriving as a fallback rather than as a recorded conclusion. **Two: `save_receipt()`'s three defaults are removed** at `repository.py:209`, so `firm_id`, `client_id` and `client_code` become required arguments. ~~**Three, not scheduled: `schema.py:79`'s `client_id TEXT DEFAULT 'UNKNOWN'` and `schema.py:78`'s `firm_id TEXT NOT NULL DEFAULT 'INTELLITAX'` stay for now**, with the reasoning below and the cheapest moment identified.~~ **Superseded the same day by amendment 116: both are scheduled, and the table is rebuilt rather than migrated.** Paul's point closed it: there is no data worth preserving, so the twelve-step procedure has nothing to protect. | Paul's rule, 2026-08-20, and it is a better formulation than the one this session proposed. **The session proposed eliminating the sentinel.** Paul's correction: handling an unrecognised sender is a legitimate requirement and needs a value to hold it, so the requirement is not "no `UNKNOWN`" but **"no unreported `UNKNOWN`"**. That distinction does the work: `UNKNOWN` as a **recorded conclusion** reports and is fine, `UNKNOWN` as a **fallback** is silent by definition. **Evidence for why it matters:** receipts `7bc79f76-a2c1-43c5-b084-0ea4d29f2218` and `eb77a92e-d4cc-4850-89e8-4590bb2ce72f` were both written with `status = ok` and no flag, so nothing distinguished them from a correctly attributed receipt. **A correction of the session's own record while establishing this: an unrecognised email sender never produces `client_id = UNKNOWN` at all.** Both branches, `app.py:699` and `app.py:1076`, log it, send the alert once, record it in `email_alerts`, move the message to `INBOX.Unknown Sender` and `continue`, writing no receipt row, which section 8.6 states independently. **So amendment 113's sentence that after step 10d `UNKNOWN` could arise only from email intake is wrong**, and the true position is that closing the folder route in `worker/intake/folder_reader.py:87` leaves no live route at all, only latent defaults. **On removing `save_receipt()`'s defaults, and the claim is enumerated rather than asserted**, which is amendment 94's rule: three call sites, all in `app.py` at 736, 921 and 1100, each printed whole with its arguments checked programmatically, and every one passes `firm_id`, `client_id` and `client_code` explicitly. So the defaults are unreached, and removing them cannot break a live caller. **`firm_id="INTELLITAX"` earns removal on its own account:** it contradicts `config.py:105`, where `DEFAULT_FIRM_ID = "FIRM001"` was made the single source by amendment 89 and corrected by amendment 93 after the four named call sites turned out to be eleven, and `INTELLITAX` matches no row in `clients.csv`. **On the schema defaults, and why they are named rather than scheduled.** SQLite's `ALTER TABLE` supports four operations, being rename table, rename column, add column and drop column, and **changing a column default is not one of them**; the documented route is the twelve-step procedure of creating a new table with the wanted shape, copying the rows, dropping the old and renaming the new. `init_db()` uses `CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS`, so it will never reshape an existing table, and every migration in this codebase is an `ALTER TABLE ADD COLUMN` from `schema.py:182` onwards, so there is no precedent for a rebuild here. The database is WAL and the pipeline holds it open and writes on every poll, so a rebuild needs the pipeline stopped and a backup taken first. **The cheapest moment is step 10d, because step 10d already changes that table by dropping `client_code`**, and one rebuild could drop that column and remove both defaults together. `DROP COLUMN` alone would probably serve for `client_code`, since no index names it: the only client-ish index is `idx_client_vendor_code`, which is on `categorisations_client_vendors` and a different table. **So it is a genuine choice between one rebuild doing three things and a `DROP COLUMN` now with a rebuild later, and it is Paul's.** |

unconstrained**, and the comment above that table is rewritten because its stated reason is wrong. **The order within the window mirrors 17.5a**: stop, back up, empty and rebuild, then change the code, then one clean cycle. **Amendment 115's "named and not scheduled" on the schema defaults is superseded: they are scheduled.** | Paul's decisions, 2026-08-20. **The decisive point is his: "there is no data here worth preserving".** The twelve-step procedure exists to preserve data, so the question was never twelve steps against a drop but whether anything in that table needed to survive, and the answer is three test receipts. **The reason a rebuild is better rather than merely easier:** a migration leaves `schema.py`'s `CREATE TABLE` declaring one shape while a live database holds another, so a fresh install and a migrated one differ. **That divergence is real now, not hypothetical:** `client_code`, `source`, `filed_path`, `duplicate_of`, `locked_at`, `filed_at`, `details`, `pipeline_version`, `receipt_ref_number`, `receipt_time` and `reason` are all present in the live database only because a migration added them. **Every removal was checked against its writers before being scheduled**, which is amendment 94's rule applied deliberately after breaking it twice earlier the same day. `save_receipt()` writes `'pending'` as a literal at `repository.py:216`, so removing the column default cannot break it. `save_extraction()` takes `currency` as a required parameter, so removing that default cannot break it either, **but the literal `"GBP"` turned out to appear twelve times in production code**, six in `app.py`, four in `worker/resolution/service.py` and two in `worker/extraction/openai_vision.py`, counted and printed by file rather than estimated, so the currency is declared in thirteen places. **Two faults found only by reading the writers.** `receipts.email_received_at` holds an RFC 2822 string for email intake and a Unix epoch integer for folder intake, because `app.py:926` passes a file mtime, so anything sorting that column across sources is wrong. And `acquire_receipt_lock()` at `repository.py:675` writes a `datetime` object rather than `.isoformat()`, **relying on Python's default datetime adapter, deprecated since Python 3.12**, while `worker/__pycache__` holds `cpython-314` bytecode. **On `resolution_events`, and this is a correction to the code's own comment.** It says `extraction_id` carries no foreign key because an outcome with no extraction row would otherwise fail to write its audit row. **A NULL foreign key value satisfies the constraint**, per SQLite's own foreign keys page, so that case was never at risk and the reason is false. The conclusion is still right for a different reason, that an audit write must not fail for a reason unrelated to the fact it records, **and that reason applies equally to `receipt_id`, which the table does constrain**, so the table held both positions at once. Enforcement is live, not decorative: `PRAGMA foreign_keys=ON` at `repository.py:18`. **A wrong reason recorded in code is worse than none**, because the next session either believes it or, on discovering the NULL claim is false, removes the decision along with its justification. **On what is preserved:** the 100 vendor mappings section 17.5 calls "real practice knowledge" are already outside the database, at `Intellibills\Exports\2026-08-18_EXPORT_categorisations_client_vendors.csv`, verified on 2026-08-20 by parsing it, 100 rows all `Client_006` with `vendor_name`, `account_name` and `times_seen`; all 100 `nominal_code` values are three-digit and therefore legacy per amendment 96, **so the asset is the vendor names and what each was decided to be, not the codes**. Note the `vendor_name` values contain U+00A0, the same character that caused the Sage `1141` drift hazard, so any future matching against that file must normalise it. **And `Clients\Paul Keating\` is not touched**, verified by listing it on 2026-08-20: four engagement letters, four proposals, `Document Requests\` and `Misc\`. Section 17.5a records that a session once called that whole folder disposable, having dropped `\Receipts` from the end of a correctly scoped path while summarising it, **which is the one irreversible mistake available in this operation.** |

### v1.22, 2026-08-22
